========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… very much. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel least like myself.

I used to have these little things that sort of anchored my week. Like softball — I’m not amazing or anything, but I used to really look forward to it, just being outside and joking around with people. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I liked going and talking and feeling connected. I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.

And at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. All the time, honestly.

It’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.

And then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.

I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.\n\nAnd at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… absolutely. That’s been one of the most noticeable things, actually.

By the time I get through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting afterward, I feel like my whole body has been filled with sand or something. It’s not just “I’m tired because work was busy.” It’s this heavy, dragging feeling, like even standing at the kitchen counter deciding what to make for dinner takes more effort than it should.

And then there are the little things that shouldn’t be hard — answering a few parent emails, packing up my bag for the next day, washing a pan, even just walking upstairs to change clothes — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “for five minutes” and then I just… can’t make myself get up. Not because I don’t know what needs doing. I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.

Like softball — I’m not amazing or anything, but I used to really look forward to it, just being outside and joking around with people. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I liked going and talking and feeling connected. I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.\n\nAnd at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely. That’s been one of the most noticeable things, actually.\n\nBy the time I get through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting afterward, I feel like my whole body has been filled with sand or something. It’s not just “I’m tired because work was busy.” It’s this heavy, dragging feeling, like even standing at the kitchen counter deciding what to make for dinner takes more effort than it should.\n\nAnd then there are the little things that shouldn’t be hard — answering a few parent emails, packing up my bag for the next day, washing a pan, even just walking upstairs to change clothes — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “for five minutes” and then I just… can’t make myself get up. Not because I don’t know what needs doing. I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.

It’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.

And I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.

I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.

But then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.

So I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.

I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.\n\nSo I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to look forward to softball, book club, my garden, and robotics club, but now I mostly go through the motions or cancel because none of it feels enjoyable anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.

Like softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.

Same with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I’ve canceled a couple times or shown up and barely said anything, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why does this feel so pointless?”

And my garden… I used to get so much satisfaction from that. Checking what was growing, planning what to plant next, even pulling weeds felt kind of calming. Now I’ll see it out there and just feel guilty, because I know I used to care and I can’t seem to make myself care in the same way.

Even robotics club at school — and I really used to enjoy that, watching the students get excited and problem-solve — lately I’m there because I’m responsible for it, not because it feels rewarding. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.

I don’t want to act on it — that’s part of why I’m here, because I don’t want to do anything — but the fact that those thoughts are coming up at all really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like hobbies or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.\n\nSo I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.\n\nLike softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I’ve canceled a couple times or shown up and barely said anything, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why does this feel so pointless?”\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get so much satisfaction from that. Checking what was growing, planning what to plant next, even pulling weeds felt kind of calming. Now I’ll see it out there and just feel guilty, because I know I used to care and I can’t seem to make myself care in the same way.\n\nEven robotics club at school — and I really used to enjoy that, watching the students get excited and problem-solve — lately I’m there because I’m responsible for it, not because it feels rewarding. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced a significant loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, not just on certain days when you're particularly stressed or busy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 8\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]

{"key":"S029","name":"Fatigue","description":"Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.","reason":"The doctor is asking directly about significant loss of energy and feeling fatigued nearly every day."}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It’s not just on the days when something obviously big happens. I mean, teaching is always tiring, so part of me keeps trying to explain it away as, “Well, of course I’m tired, I was on my feet all day,” but this feels heavier than that.

By the time I get through classes and then, say, a department meeting, my body feels like it’s made of wet cement or something. I’ll get home and even really basic things — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, putting things away — feel like I’m climbing a hill. Not impossible exactly, because I usually can force myself through some of it, but it takes this ridiculous amount of effort.

And it’s not just a certain “busy day” kind of tired. It feels like my energy is already low before I even start, and then the normal parts of the day just drain whatever little bit is there. So yes, nearly every day lately, I’d say I’ve felt fatigued in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.

It’s hard to say out loud, because I’m a teacher and I’m used to being the person who keeps everything together, but lately I catch myself thinking that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then sometimes, especially when I’m driving home after school, my mind sort of wanders to this awful place where I wonder whether my family, or even my students, would be better off without me. I don’t want to act on it — that’s part of why I’m here, because I don’t want to do anything — but the fact that those thoughts are coming up at all really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like hobbies or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel least like myself.\n\nI used to have these little things that sort of anchored my week. Like softball — I’m not amazing or anything, but I used to really look forward to it, just being outside and joking around with people. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I liked going and talking and feeling connected. I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.\n\nAnd at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely. That’s been one of the most noticeable things, actually.\n\nBy the time I get through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting afterward, I feel like my whole body has been filled with sand or something. It’s not just “I’m tired because work was busy.” It’s this heavy, dragging feeling, like even standing at the kitchen counter deciding what to make for dinner takes more effort than it should.\n\nAnd then there are the little things that shouldn’t be hard — answering a few parent emails, packing up my bag for the next day, washing a pan, even just walking upstairs to change clothes — they feel like climbing a hill. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.\n\nLike softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I’ve canceled a couple times or shown up and barely said anything, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why does this feel so pointless?”\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get so much satisfaction from that. Checking what was growing, planning what to plant next, even pulling weeds felt kind of calming. Now I’ll see it out there and just feel guilty, because I know I used to care and I can’t seem to make myself care in the same way.\n\nEven robotics club at school — and I really used to enjoy that, watching the students get excited and problem-solve — lately I’m there because I’m responsible for it, not because it feels rewarding. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, not just on certain days when you're particularly stressed or busy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not just on the days when something obviously big happens. I mean, teaching is always tiring, so part of me keeps trying to explain it away as, “Well, of course I’m tired, I was on my feet all day,” but this feels heavier than that.\n\nBy the time I get through classes and then, say, a department meeting, my body feels like it’s made of wet cement or something. It feels like my energy is already low before I even start, and then the normal parts of the day just drain whatever little bit is there. I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone has, where I’ve been stressed or tired or not really myself for a while. Teaching has had seasons where I’ve felt worn down, especially when the workload piles up, and there have definitely been times I’ve thought, “I need a break,” or “I’m not enjoying things as much right now.”

But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve had this exact combination before — the low mood, and the lack of interest, and the tiredness all kind of sitting on top of each other like this. At least, not in a way that I clearly remember and could point to and say, “Yes, this happened before just like this.” Part of me keeps trying to compare it to normal burnout or just being busy, but this feels… different enough that I noticed it.

So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear previous episode in mind. I’ve been down before, but I don’t know that it has felt this persistent or this hard to shake.

It’s not just on the days when something obviously big happens. I mean, teaching is always tiring, so part of me keeps trying to explain it away as, “Well, of course I’m tired, I was on my feet all day,” but this feels heavier than that.\n\nBy the time I get through classes and then, say, a department meeting, my body feels like it’s made of wet cement or something. I’ll get home and even really basic things — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, putting things away — feel like I’m climbing a hill. Not impossible exactly, because I usually can force myself through some of it, but it takes this ridiculous amount of effort.\n\nAnd it’s not just a certain “busy day” kind of tired. It feels like my energy is already low before I even start, and then the normal parts of the day just drain whatever little bit is there. So yes, nearly every day lately, I’d say I’ve felt fatigued in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before — feeling down, losing interest in things, and feeling so tired — or is this the first time you’ve felt this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone has, where I’ve been stressed or tired or not really myself for a while. Teaching has had seasons where I’ve felt worn down, especially when the workload piles up, and there have definitely been times I’ve thought, “I need a break,” or “I’m not enjoying things as much right now.”\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I’ve had this exact combination before — the low mood, and the lack of interest, and the tiredness all kind of sitting on top of each other like this. At least, not in a way that I clearly remember and could point to and say, “Yes, this happened before just like this.” Part of me keeps trying to compare it to normal burnout or just being busy, but this feels… different enough that I noticed it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear previous episode in mind. I’ve been down before, but I don’t know that it has felt this persistent or this hard to shake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think back, and… I honestly don’t have a clear memory of a time that was really like this for several weeks or more. I mean, there were definitely periods where I was exhausted or discouraged, especially with school years where everything felt overloaded, but I’m not sure that was the same thing.

Like, I can remember times where I was tired and kind of emotionally wrung out, and maybe I wasn’t as excited about things because I was busy or stressed. But when I try to compare it to now — this combination of being down, not enjoying things, and feeling unusually tired almost all the time — I don’t know. Nothing jumps out as, “Yes, that definitely happened before.”

Part of the problem is I’m probably not the best judge of it looking backward. I tend to explain things away at the time, like, “Oh, it’s just the semester,” or “I’m just overcommitted,” or “Once this busy stretch ends I’ll feel normal again.” So maybe there were times that were closer than I’m remembering, but I can’t honestly point to one and say it lasted several weeks and felt like this.

So… I guess my answer is still that I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s an obvious earlier episode I can identify, at least not one that stands out clearly.

I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely. I’ll sit down “for five minutes” and then I just… can’t make myself get up. Not because I don’t know what needs doing. I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.\n\nSo I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.\n\nLike softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, not just on certain days when you're particularly stressed or busy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I know you’re asking for a clearer yes or no, and I’m trying to be really honest, but I still don’t think I can point to a specific time and say, yes, that was definitely the same thing for two weeks or more.

Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s hard to say out loud, because I’m a teacher and I’m used to being the person who keeps everything together, but lately I catch myself thinking that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then sometimes, especially when I’m driving home after school, my mind sort of wanders to this awful place where I wonder whether my family, or even my students, would be better off without me. I don’t want to act on it — that’s part of why I’m here, because I don’t want to do anything — but the fact that those thoughts are coming up at all really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like hobbies or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel least like myself.\n\nI used to have these little things that sort of anchored my week. Like softball — I’m not amazing or anything, but I used to really look forward to it, just being outside and joking around with people. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I liked going and talking and feeling connected. I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.\n\nAnd at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely. That’s been one of the most noticeable things, actually.\n\nBy the time I get through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting afterward, I feel like my whole body has been filled with sand or something. It’s not just “I’m tired because work was busy.” It’s this heavy, dragging feeling, like even standing at the kitchen counter deciding what to make for dinner takes more effort than it should.\n\nAnd then there are the little things that shouldn’t be hard — answering a few parent emails, packing up my bag for the next day, washing a pan, even just walking upstairs to change clothes — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “for five minutes” and then I just… can’t make myself get up. Not because I don’t know what needs doing. I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.\n\nSo I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.\n\nLike softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I’ve canceled a couple times or shown up and barely said anything, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why does this feel so pointless?”\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get so much satisfaction from that. Checking what was growing, planning what to plant next, even pulling weeds felt kind of calming. Now I’ll see it out there and just feel guilty, because I know I used to care and I can’t seem to make myself care in the same way.\n\nEven robotics club at school — and I really used to enjoy that, watching the students get excited and problem-solve — lately I’m there because I’m responsible for it, not because it feels rewarding. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, not just on certain days when you're particularly stressed or busy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not just on the days when something obviously big happens. I mean, teaching is always tiring, so part of me keeps trying to explain it away as, “Well, of course I’m tired, I was on my feet all day,” but this feels heavier than that.\n\nBy the time I get through classes and then, say, a department meeting, my body feels like it’s made of wet cement or something. I’ll get home and even really basic things — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, putting things away — feel like I’m climbing a hill. Not impossible exactly, because I usually can force myself through some of it, but it takes this ridiculous amount of effort.\n\nAnd it’s not just a certain “busy day” kind of tired. It feels like my energy is already low before I even start, and then the normal parts of the day just drain whatever little bit is there. So yes, nearly every day lately, I’d say I’ve felt fatigued in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before — feeling down, losing interest in things, and feeling so tired — or is this the first time you’ve felt this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone has, where I’ve been stressed or tired or not really myself for a while. Teaching has had seasons where I’ve felt worn down, especially when the workload piles up, and there have definitely been times I’ve thought, “I need a break,” or “I’m not enjoying things as much right now.”\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I’ve had this exact combination before — the low mood, and the lack of interest, and the tiredness all kind of sitting on top of each other like this. At least, not in a way that I clearly remember and could point to and say, “Yes, this happened before just like this.” Part of me keeps trying to compare it to normal burnout or just being busy, but this feels… different enough that I noticed it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear previous episode in mind. I’ve been down before, but I don’t know that it has felt this persistent or this hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back further — maybe even years ago — to see if there was ever a time when you felt this way for several weeks or more, where you were down, lost interest in things, and felt unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… I honestly don’t have a clear memory of a time that was really like this for several weeks or more. I mean, there were definitely periods where I was exhausted or discouraged, especially with school years where everything felt overloaded, but I’m not sure that was the same thing.\n\nLike, I can remember times where I was tired and kind of emotionally wrung out, and maybe I wasn’t as excited about things because I was busy or stressed. But when I try to compare it to now — this combination of being down, not enjoying things, and feeling unusually tired almost all the time — I don’t know. Nothing jumps out as, “Yes, that definitely happened before.”\n\nPart of the problem is I’m probably not the best judge of it looking backward. I tend to explain things away at the time, like, “Oh, it’s just the semester,” or “I’m just overcommitted,” or “Once this busy stretch ends I’ll feel normal again.” So maybe there were times that were closer than I’m remembering, but I can’t honestly point to one and say it lasted several weeks and felt like this.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is still that I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s an obvious earlier episode I can identify, at least not one that stands out clearly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back now to any time in your life — even if it was years ago — when you felt down, lost interest in things, and felt unusually tired for two weeks or more in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for a clearer yes or no, and I’m trying to be really honest, but I still don’t think I can point to a specific time and say, yes, that was definitely the same thing for two weeks or more.\n\nWhen I look back, there were definitely times I felt run down or low, especially during stressful stretches, and I’m sure there were weeks where I wasn’t enjoying much because I was overwhelmed or just exhausted. But I don’t know if that was this same kind of down feeling, with losing interest and feeling unusually tired all together, every day or nearly every day.\n\nNothing really stands out in my memory as a distinct period like that. I may have brushed things off at the time, so I don’t want to say it never happened with total certainty, but I can’t remember a clear episode like that before. Right now feels more noticeable and harder to explain away than what I remember from the past.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I… I guess I’m here because my thoughts have started scaring me. It’s hard to say out loud, because I’m a teacher and I’m used to being the person who keeps everything together, but lately I catch myself thinking that maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd then sometimes, especially when I’m driving home after school, my mind sort of wanders to this awful place where I wonder whether my family, or even my students, would be better off without me. I don’t want to act on it — that’s part of why I’m here, because I don’t want to do anything — but the fact that those thoughts are coming up at all really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like hobbies or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel least like myself.\n\nI used to have these little things that sort of anchored my week. Like softball — I’m not amazing or anything, but I used to really look forward to it, just being outside and joking around with people. And book club, even when I hadn’t finished the book, I liked going and talking and feeling connected. I have a garden too, or I guess I had one I cared about… I used to check on it almost obsessively, like the tomatoes and herbs and all that, and it used to make me feel calm.\n\nAnd at school I help with the robotics club, and that used to be one of the parts of teaching that gave me energy, watching the kids get excited when something finally worked. But now I mostly just… go through the motions. I show up because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to, but I don’t feel that enjoyment. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. All the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like every small mistake turns into evidence that I’m failing at everything. If a lesson goes badly — even just a normal bad day, like the students are restless or I don’t explain something clearly — I don’t just think, “That didn’t go well.” I think, “There it is. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely. That’s been one of the most noticeable things, actually.\n\nBy the time I get through a full day of teaching and then, say, a department meeting afterward, I feel like my whole body has been filled with sand or something. It’s not just “I’m tired because work was busy.” It’s this heavy, dragging feeling, like even standing at the kitchen counter deciding what to make for dinner takes more effort than it should.\n\nAnd then there are the little things that shouldn’t be hard — answering a few parent emails, packing up my bag for the next day, washing a pan, even just walking upstairs to change clothes — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “for five minutes” and then I just… can’t make myself get up. Not because I don’t know what needs doing. I know exactly what needs doing, and that almost makes it worse, because I’m staring at these normal adult responsibilities and they feel enormous.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, not just when something bad happens or during particularly stressful times?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I do feel low a lot, but when you say “most of the day, nearly every day,” I start second-guessing myself, because I don’t know if I’m tracking it that precisely.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m actively crying or anything dramatic. Sometimes it’s more like this dull heaviness in the background, and I only notice it when I realize I haven’t felt normal for a while. But I don’t know whether that counts as “most of the day” or whether it’s more that certain parts of the day feel worse than others.\n\nAnd I guess I’m also not sure what counts as being “down” versus just being tired or stressed or… I don’t know, worn out. So yes, I’ve noticed my mood being lower than I’d expect, but I don’t think I can confidently say exactly how often in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past couple of weeks — have there been many days where you felt down or in a low mood for most of the day, even when nothing particularly bad was happening?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think about it honestly, because I don’t want to overstate it just because I feel awful right now. Over the past couple of weeks… yes, there have been a lot of days where I’ve felt low, but I still get stuck on “most of the day.” Like, I can function through parts of the day, especially at school, because I have to. There are bells ringing, students asking questions, things happening one after another, so I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I feel sad.” Sometimes I’m just moving through it.\n\nBut then if I actually look back, the feeling is kind of there underneath even when nothing specific has happened. It’s like I wake up already bracing myself, and even if the day is completely ordinary, there’s this grayness to it. Not necessarily crying or anything obvious, just… flat, heavy, not myself. And it follows me around more than I want to admit.\n\nSo I guess the answer is probably yes, many days. I just don’t know how to measure it perfectly. It’s not always sharp, but it’s there enough that I notice I’m not having many days that feel normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you no longer take pleasure in almost anything, or that even things you used to really enjoy feel completely flat or meaningless to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one is hard to admit, but yes. I don’t think it’s that I literally feel nothing about absolutely everything, but the things that used to feel like mine — the things I actually looked forward to — feel really flat now.\n\nLike softball, for example. I used to be excited about it during the week, even if I was tired after teaching. I liked being outside, seeing the team, having that hour where I wasn’t “Ms. whatever,” I was just myself. Lately I either drag myself there and kind of go through the motions, or I find some reason not to go. And when I do go, I’m not really enjoying it. I’m just counting down until I can leave, which makes me feel awful because nothing bad is happening there.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love having the book half-read and then rushing to finish it, talking about it with people, making snacks, all of that. Now I’ll look at the book and it’s like the words just don’t matter to me. I’ve canceled a couple times or shown up and barely said anything, and I’m sitting there thinking, “Why does this feel so pointless?”\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get so much satisfaction from that. Checking what was growing, planning what to plant next, even pulling weeds felt kind of calming. Now I’ll see it out there and just feel guilty, because I know I used to care and I can’t seem to make myself care in the same way.\n\nEven robotics club at school — and I really used to enjoy that, watching the students get excited and problem-solve — lately I’m there because I’m responsible for it, not because it feels rewarding. I still do what I’m supposed to do, but inside it’s like the enjoyable part has been switched off. Everything feels like obligation, not pleasure.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant loss of energy or felt fatigued nearly every day, not just on certain days when you're particularly stressed or busy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not just on the days when something obviously big happens. I mean, teaching is always tiring, so part of me keeps trying to explain it away as, “Well, of course I’m tired, I was on my feet all day,” but this feels heavier than that.\n\nBy the time I get through classes and then, say, a department meeting, my body feels like it’s made of wet cement or something. I’ll get home and even really basic things — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, putting things away — feel like I’m climbing a hill. Not impossible exactly, because I usually can force myself through some of it, but it takes this ridiculous amount of effort.\n\nAnd it’s not just a certain “busy day” kind of tired. It feels like my energy is already low before I even start, and then the normal parts of the day just drain whatever little bit is there. So yes, nearly every day lately, I’d say I’ve felt fatigued in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you had experiences like this before — feeling down, losing interest in things, and feeling so tired — or is this the first time you’ve felt this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone has, where I’ve been stressed or tired or not really myself for a while. Teaching has had seasons where I’ve felt worn down, especially when the workload piles up, and there have definitely been times I’ve thought, “I need a break,” or “I’m not enjoying things as much right now.”\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I’ve had this exact combination before — the low mood, and the lack of interest, and the tiredness all kind of sitting on top of each other like this. At least, not in a way that I clearly remember and could point to and say, “Yes, this happened before just like this.” Part of me keeps trying to compare it to normal burnout or just being busy, but this feels… different enough that I noticed it.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear previous episode in mind. I’ve been down before, but I don’t know that it has felt this persistent or this hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back further — maybe even years ago — to see if there was ever a time when you felt this way for several weeks or more, where you were down, lost interest in things, and felt unusually tired?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back, and… I honestly don’t have a clear memory of a time that was really like this for several weeks or more. I mean, there were definitely periods where I was exhausted or discouraged, especially with school years where everything felt overloaded, but I’m not sure that was the same thing.\n\nLike, I can remember times where I was tired and kind of emotionally wrung out, and maybe I wasn’t as excited about things because I was busy or stressed. But when I try to compare it to now — this combination of being down, not enjoying things, and feeling unusually tired almost all the time — I don’t know. Nothing jumps out as, “Yes, that definitely happened before.”\n\nPart of the problem is I’m probably not the best judge of it looking backward. I tend to explain things away at the time, like, “Oh, it’s just the semester,” or “I’m just overcommitted,” or “Once this busy stretch ends I’ll feel normal again.” So maybe there were times that were closer than I’m remembering, but I can’t honestly point to one and say it lasted several weeks and felt like this.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is still that I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s an obvious earlier episode I can identify, at least not one that stands out clearly.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back now to any time in your life — even if it was years ago — when you felt down, lost interest in things, and felt unusually tired for two weeks or more in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for a clearer yes or no, and I’m trying to be really honest, but I still don’t think I can point to a specific time and say, yes, that was definitely the same thing for two weeks or more.\n\nWhen I look back, there were definitely times I felt run down or low, especially during stressful stretches, and I’m sure there were weeks where I wasn’t enjoying much because I was overwhelmed or just exhausted. But I don’t know if that was this same kind of down feeling, with losing interest and feeling unusually tired all together, every day or nearly every day.\n\nNothing really stands out in my memory as a distinct period like that. I may have brushed things off at the time, so I don’t want to say it never happened with total certainty, but I can’t remember a clear episode like that before. Right now feels more noticeable and harder to explain away than what I remember from the past.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back now to any time in your life — even if it was years ago — when you felt down, lost interest in things, and felt unusually tired for two weeks or more in a row?\n\nPatient: I know I’m kind of circling the same answer, and I’m sorry — I’m not trying to be difficult. I just don’t have a clean memory of a time where I could say, “Yes, for two weeks or more I was down, I lost interest, and I was unusually tired,” all together in that way.\n\nThere were periods where I was worn out, definitely. Teaching can blur together sometimes, especially during stressful terms, and I can remember stretches where I probably wasn’t very enthusiastic about much. But I keep hesitating because I don’t know if that was actually the same as what you’re describing, or if it was just being overloaded and needing rest.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I can identify a previous episode like this, I don’t think I can. I can’t say with certainty that it never happened, because looking back is messy, but nothing specific comes to mind that I’d feel confident naming.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m sorry, I know you’ve asked this a few times and I’m probably not giving you the kind of definite answer you’re looking for. I’m really trying to search my memory properly.

I don’t think I can identify a clear period like that, no. Not one where I can confidently say, “For at least two weeks, I was down, I wasn’t interested in things I usually enjoy, and I felt unusually tired,” all together in a way that stood out.

I don’t want to act on it — that’s part of why I’m here, because I don’t want to do anything — but the fact that those thoughts are coming up at all really frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, like hobbies or time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel least like myself.\n\nI used to have these little things that sort of anchored my week. A lot of the time I cancel if I can, especially book club or softball, because the idea of going feels pointless, and then when I do go I’m just kind of there, waiting for it to be over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a burden to others, or that you don’t measure up to who you feel you should be?\n\nPatient: Yes. You’re not good at this. They deserve someone better.” And if one student looks confused, even if it’s probably just because the material is hard or they’re tired, I take it so personally. I’ll replay their face later, over and over, like I should have done more, planned better, noticed sooner.\n\nAnd then it bleeds into everything else. I’ll go home still thinking about it and somehow it becomes, “I’m letting my students down, I’m letting my family down, I’m not the kind of mother I should be.” I know, logically, people have off days and no teacher is perfect, but emotionally it feels like proof that I’m just… not enough. Like everyone is carrying extra weight because I can’t hold up my end.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired than usual, or that even small tasks take a lot more energy than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… absolutely.
